<span style="color:#800000">fta504</span> “Qu’adonc ils entrerent en ferme et paisible possession de l’honneur que Dieu leur a fait par dessus les autres nations.” — *Fr.* <span style="color:#800000">fta505</span> “Car a quelle fin font produits des fruits de tant de sortes, et en telle abondance, et qu’il y a tant de vieux de plaisance, si non pour l’usage et commodite des hommes ?” — *Fr.* <span style="color:#800000">fta506</span> “C’est contre la nature des deux elemens.” — *Fr.* <span style="color:#800000">fta507</span> “Il n’en fait yci que bien petite mention et comme en passant.” — *Fr.* “He here only slightly adverts to this subject, and as it were in passing.”

But the text writing <span style="color:#008080">w</span> not <span style="color:#008080">y</span>, and the context agreeing with it, the punctuation must, in reason, give place; and, accordingly, all the ancient interpreters appear to have read it <span style="color:#008080">wçpn</span>*, his soul,* meaning by that *his own soul,* or the *soul of the swearer.”* <span style="color:#800000">fta511</span> “*Asfavoir, Jacob.” — Fr.* “Namely, Jacob.”

<span style="color:#800000">fta514</span> The Septuagint reads, <span style="color:#0000d4">Arate πύλὰς οἱ άρχοντες ὑμῶν</span>, which may be rendered, “Ye princes, lift up your gates.” The reading of the Vulgate is similar:“Attollite portas principes vestras” and so is that of the Arabic and Ethiopic. But that rendering, as Calvin justly Observes, inadmissible; for in the Hebrew text, the affix <span style="color:#008080">µk</span>, *kem, your,* is joined to <span style="color:#008080">yçar</span>, *roshey, heads,* and not to <span style="color:#008080">µyr[ç</span>, *shearim, gates.* Although, however the reading of the Septuagint may be translated as above, “Ye princes, lift up your gates,” Hammond thinks it more probable, that the translators intended <span style="color:#0000d4">οἱ άρχοντες ὑμῶν</span>*, your princes,* to represent <span style="color:#008080">µkyçar</span>, *rashekem,* as myertin, by mistake, the construction of the sentence, so *your heads,* as to give this reading, “Your heads, or princes, lift up the gates, instead of, “Ye gates, lift up your heads.”

He reads, “His soul shall rest in bliss;” and has the following note:— “<span style="color:#008080">˚ylt</span>, *pernoctavit.* The words seem to allude to the happy state of the good man’s departed soul, while his posterity prosper in the present world.” That is, the land of Canaan, which God promised to perpetuate to the obedient Israelites and their posterity. “It was promised and given,” says Poole, “as an earnest of the whole covenant of grace and all his promises, and, therefore, is synecdochically put for all of them. The sense is, his seed shall be blessed.”

David was now deserted, desolate, and destitute of all help. The word is used in the same sense in Psalm 22:20, and 35:17.

<p class="title-block-h2" style="text-align:center; font-size:16px; font-weight:bold; margin:18px 0 12px;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#006411">PSALM 26</span></p>

<!-- PAGE 1228 -->
<span style="color:#800000">fta531</span> Hammond renders the original word, “Plead for, or defend me;” and Green, “Vindicate me.” The word denotes both the act of a judge and of an advocate. This last view agrees very well with the scope of the psalm, which, from the strong assertions of innocence with which it abounds, appears to have been written by David in vindication of himself from various crimes which had been alleged against him; although the particular events to which it refers are not indicated.

<span style="color:#800000">fta532</span> The primary signification of the Hebrew word <span style="color:#008080">ãrx</span>, *tsaraph* is to try as the refiner tries his gold by dissolving and melting it, In this sense it is used in Psalm 66:10, “Thou hast tried us as silver is tried.”

<span style="color:#800000">fta533</span> Horsley renders the word, “Those who seek concealment.” In like manner, the Chaldee paraphrases it, “They that hide themselves that they may do evil.”

<span style="color:#800000">fta534</span> *“Innocence.” — Fr. marg* <span style="color:#800000">fta535</span> The washing of the hands in solemn protestation of innocence, on particular occasions, was enjoined by the Mosaic ritual, and was common among the Jews, Deuteronomy 21:6, 7. It was in common use among them before prayer; and the priests, in particular, were not to perform any sacred office in the sanctuary until they had poured water from the laver, which was set in the temple for that purpose, and washed their hands, Exodus 40:30-33.

<span style="color:#800000">fta536</span> Mudge conjectures that the expression, *encompass,* is probably taken from the custom of forming a ring round the altar at the time of worship. And Goodwyn informs us, that at the feast of tabernacles the people, on the seventh day, encompassed the altar seven times, carrying branches of palm trees in their hands in remembrance of the overthrow of Jericho, and singing hosannas. — *Moses and Aaron,* p. 132. David, however, may refer to the practice of the priests, who, when they offered sacrifices, went round about the altar; and his meaning may simply be, that as the priests first washed their hands, and then performed their sacred office at the altar; so he deeply felt the necessity of personal purity, in order to his engaging in the service of God.

<span style="color:#800000">fta537</span> *The habitation of thy house —* a Hebraism for *the house which thou inhabitest.* This name was given to the tabernacle, 1 Samuel 2:29, 32, and afterwards to Solomon’s temple, 2 Chronicles 36:15. <span style="color:#800000">fta538</span> Hebrew:— *men of blood.* See Psalm 5:7.

<!-- PAGE 1229 -->
<span style="color:#800000">fta539</span> Namely <span style="color:#008080">˚y[</span>, *ayin.* <span style="color:#800000">fta540</span> The word which it employs is <span style="color:#0000d4">ἐυπρέρπεια</span>.

<span style="color:#800000">fta541</span> “C’est, en lieu plain et droict; c’est a dire, seur.” — *Fr*. *marg.* “That is, in a plain and straight; that is to say, sure place.”

<span style="color:#800000">fta542</span> “Qu’elle soit celebree publiquement; afin qu’elle serve d’exempleaux autres pour se confermer en Dieu.” — *Fr*.

<p class="title-block-h2" style="text-align:center; font-size:16px; font-weight:bold; margin:18px 0 12px;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#006411">PSALM 27</span></p>

<span style="color:#800000">fta543</span> The rendering of the learned Castellio is, “Si invadant — offensuri sunt atque casuri;” — “If they invade me they shall stumble and fall. The Hebrew verbs for “stumble” and “fall”

<span style="color:#800000">fta544</span> French and Skinner read, “to devour my flesh;” and observe, that “this image is taken from a wild beast. Compare Psalm 3:7, and Psalm 22:13.”

<span style="color:#800000">fta545</span> “C’est, d’adversite.” *— Note, Fr. marg.* “That is, of adversity.”

<span style="color:#800000">fta546</span> “Sous le regne de Christ.” — *Fr*.

<span style="color:#800000">fta547</span> “Sacrificia jubili.” *— Lat.* “Sacrifice de triomphe.” — *Fr*. Ainsworth reads, “Sacrifices of shouting, or of triumph, of joyful sounding and alarm.” “This,” says he, “hath respect to the law which appointed over the sacrifices trumpets to be sounded, Numbers 10:10, whose chiefest, most loud, joyful, and triumphant sound was called *trughnah,* [or <span style="color:#008080">h[wrt</span>, *truah,* the word here used,] ‘triumph,’ ‘alarm,’ or ‘jubilation,’ Numbers 10:5-7.”

<span style="color:#800000">fta548</span> “Ou dit de toy.” — *Fr*. *marg.* “Or said of or concerning thee.”

<span style="color:#800000">fta549</span> “*Pourtant.” — Fr.* <span style="color:#800000">fta550</span> Calvin’s meaning appears to be this:- God has given us in his word that gracious command or invitation, “Seek ye my face,” inviting us to seek him by prayer and the other exercises of religion. Now, when David says, “My heart said to thee, Seek ye my face,” he means that his heart reminded God of his command or invitation; and by this he encouraged himself to seek God’s face, which he expresses his resolution to do in the following clause, “Thy face, O Jehovah! will I seek.”

<span style="color:#800000">fta551</span> “C’est, plaisir.” — *Fr*. *marg.* “That is, will or pleasure.”

<!-- PAGE 1230 -->

<span style="color:#800000">fta552</span> “En la sauvegarde et protection de Dieu.” — *Fr*.

<span style="color:#800000">fta553</span> Hammond renders the words “breathers or *speakers of injury* or *rapine;* <span style="color:#008080">smj</span>, signifying *injury* or *rapine,* and <span style="color:#008080">jwp</span>, *speak.”* Ainsworth reads, “He that breatheth or puffeth out violent wrong.

<span style="color:#800000">fta554</span> “De glaives et autre tels efforts.” — *Fr*. “From the sword and other such weapons.”

<span style="color:#800000">fta555</span> In the Hebrew this verse is elliptical, as Calvin here translates it. In the French version he supplies the ellipsis, by adding to the end of the verse the words, “C’estoit fait de moy,” “I had perished.” In our English version, the words, “I had fainted,” are introduced as a supplement, in the beginning of the verse. Both the supplement of Calvin, and that of our English version, which are substantially the same, doubtless explain the meaning of the passage; but they destroy the elegant abrupt form of the expression employed by the Psalmist, who breaks off in the middle of his discourse without completing the sentence, although what he meant to say is very evident. “Unless I had believed to see the goodness of the Lord in the land of the living, What! what, alas! should have become of me!” - *Dr Adam Clarke.* As, however, <span style="color:#008080">alwl</span>, *lulë,* which is rendered *unless,* is omitted by the ancient versions and several MSS., some consider it an interpolation, and translate the verse without an ellipsis. Thus Walford renders it, “I have believed that I shall behold the goodness of Jehovah in the land of the living.”

<span style="color:#800000">fta556</span> “A ce que sa foy ne soit jamais esbranier” — *Fr.* “That his faith might never be shaken.”

<span style="color:#800000">fta557</span> Calvin here seems to use the Septuagint version. What he renders in the text, “Be of good courage,” is rendered by the Septuagint, <span style="color:#0000d4">ἀνδρίζου</span> “Be manly, or act like a man.” The Vulgate reads, “vinliter a,,e,” following the Septuagint, as it generally does. Paul uses the same phraseology in 1 Corinthians 16:13. “These,” says Ainsworth, “are the words of encouragement against remissness, fear, faintness of heart, or other infirmities.”

<!-- PAGE 1231 -->

<p class="title-block-h2" style="text-align:center; font-size:16px; font-weight:bold; margin:18px 0 12px;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#006411">PSALM 28</span></p>

<span style="color:#800000">fta558</span> <span style="color:#008080">rybd</span>, *debir,* is derived from <span style="color:#008080">rbd</span>, *dabar, to speak.* <span style="color:#800000">fta559</span> The verb <span style="color:#008080">˚çm</span>, *mashak,* here rendered *draw,* “signifies,” as Hammond observes, “both to draw and apprehend,” and may “be best rendered here, *Seize not on me,* as he that seizeth on any to carry or drag him to execution. The Septuagint, after having literally rendered the Hebrew by <span style="color:#0000d4">Μὴ συνελκύσῃς thn ψυχήν mou</span>, *draw not my soul together with, etc.,* adds <span style="color:#0000d4">Κίαν μὴ συναπολέσῃς me</span> etc., *and destroy me not together with, etc.* Calvin here evidently takes the same view; though he does not express it in the form of criticism.

<span style="color:#800000">fta560</span> “Que ceste finesse de renard, quand on use de beaux semblans pour avoir occasion de nuire.” — *Fr.* <span style="color:#800000">fta561</span> “Voulant devorer les agneaux.” — *Fr.* <span style="color:#800000">fta562</span> “Conduisant et gouvernant toutes choses.” — *Fr.* <span style="color:#800000">fta563</span> “C’estoyent certains peuples ou escrimeurs qui souloyent ainsi comme etre. Voyez les Chiliades d’Erasme.” *— Note, Fr. marg.* “These were certain people or fencers, who were wont to fight in this manner. See the Chiliades of Erasmus.”

<span style="color:#800000">fta564</span> “He will destroy them, and not build them up.”

<span style="color:#800000">fta565</span> “Que tout la prospetite qu’ils se souhaitent soit a cause du peuple. — *Fr.* “That all the prosperity they desire should be for the sake of the people.”

<span style="color:#800000">fta566</span> “Veu que la plus grand part rejette et desdaigne de porter le joug de Christ.” — *Fr.* <span style="color:#800000">fta567</span> “Qu’il n’en est pas digne.” — *Fr.* “That he is not worthy of it.”

<span style="color:#800000">fta568</span> “C’est, digne de son nom.” *— Note, Fr. Marg.* “That is, worthy of his name.”

<span style="color:#800000">fta569</span> The entire reading of the verse in the Septuagint is, “<span style="color:#0000d4">Ενέγκατε τῳ</span> <span style="color:#0000d4">Κυρίῳ ὑιοὶ Θεοῦ ενέγκατε τῳ Κυρίῳ ὑιοὺς κριῶν</span>” “Bring to the Lord, ye sons of God, bring to the Lord young rams.” Thus the LXX, as is not unusual in other places, render the words for “Ye sons of the mighty” twice; first, in the vocative case, addressing them, <span style="color:#0000d4">Υιοὶ</span>

<!-- PAGE 1232 -->

<span style="color:#0000d4">Qeou</span>*, Ye sons of God,* and then in the accusative case, <span style="color:#0000d4">ὑιοὺς κριῶν</span>, *young rams,* being apparently doubtful which was the correct rendering, and, therefore, putting down both. The Vulgate, Arabic, and Ethiopic, exactly follow them. Jerome also reads, “Afferte Domino filios arietum;” although he does not give a double translation of the original words. But the correct rendering, we have no doubt, is, “Ye sons of the mighty;” which is just a Hebrew idiomatic expression for “Ye mighty ones,” or, “Ye princes;” and to them the inspired writer addresses an invitation to acknowledge and worship God from the manifestation of his majesty and power in the wonders of nature.

<span style="color:#800000">fta570</span> The Hebrew word which Calvin renders *“mighty,”* is <span style="color:#008080">µyla</span>, *elym*, a word which means *gods.* The Hebrew word <span style="color:#008080">µylya</span>, *eylim,* which means *rams,* nearly resembles it, having only an additional <span style="color:#008080">y</span>, *yod,* and this letter is often cut off in nouns.

<span style="color:#800000">fta571</span> The Chaldee paraphrases it thus:— “The assembly of angels, sons of God,” meaning by God *angels.* <span style="color:#800000">fta572</span> This translation conveys a somewhat different meaning from that of our English version; but it is supported by several critics. Green reads, “In his beautiful sanctuary;” and Fry, “Worship Jehovah with holy reverence,” or, “Worship Jehovah in the glorious places of the sanctuary.” “Where the Hebrews read <span style="color:#008080">trdhb</span>” says Hammond, “in *the glory* or *beauty* of *holiness,* from <span style="color:#008080">rdh</span>, *to honor,* or *beautify,* the LXX. read, <span style="color:#0000d4">ἐν αὐλῇ ἁγίᾳ aujtou</span>*, in his holy court,* as if it were from, “*penetrale, thalamus, area*, a closet, a marriage-chamber, a court; and so the Latin and Syriac follow them, and the Arabic, in his *“holy habitation.”* <span style="color:#800000">fta573</span> Dr Francis’ Translation of Horace.

<span style="color:#800000">fta574</span> “Qui contraignent les barbares et gens esbestez sentir qu’il y a un Dieu.” — *Fr.* “Which constrain the rude and insensible to feel that there is a God.”

<span style="color:#800000">fta575</span> That is, the wilderness of Zin, Numbers 33:86. It is described in Deuteronomy 1:19, as the “great and terrible wilderness.” The Israelites passed through this wilderness in their way from Egypt to the promised land, Numbers 13:27. It received its name from the city of Kadesh, by which it lay, Numbers 20:1, 16.

<!-- PAGE 1233 -->

<span style="color:#800000">fta576</span> The Sidonians applied to Hermon the name of Sirion, Deuteronomy 3:9.

<span style="color:#800000">fta577</span> “D’approcher de Dieu.” — *Fr.* <span style="color:#800000">fta578</span> “Fait avortir.” — *Fr.* “To miscarry or prove abortive.”

<span style="color:#800000">fta579</span> Bishop Lowth reads, “Maketh the oaks to tremble,” (Lectures on Hebrew Poetry, vol. 2:p.253,) in which he is followed by Dimock, Green, Seeker, Horsley, Fry, and others. But Dathe, Berlin, De Rossi, Dr Adam Clarke, Rogers, etc., adhere to the common interpretation, in which they are supported by all the ancient versions, except the Syriac, which seems to favor the view of Lowth. A main argument of Lowth and those who follow him in support of his rendering is, that the common translation, which supposes the passage to relate to *the hinds bring forth their young,* agrees very little with the rest of the imagery either in nature or dignity; whereas the oak struck with lightning, is a far nobler image, and one which falls in more naturally with the scattering of a forest’s foliage under the action of a storm. But Rogers justly observes, that “we are not warranted in altering the Hebrew text, because the oriental imagery which we meet with does not correspond with our ideas of poetical beauty and grandeur,” (Book of Psalms in Hebrew, metrically arranged, vol. it. p. 186.) With respect to the sense conveyed by the common reading, it may be observed, that birds bring forth their young with great difficulty and pain, bowing themselves, bruising their young ones, and casting out their sorrows, (Job 39:4, 6;) and it therefore heightens the description given of the terrific character of the thunder-storm, when the thunder, which is here called the voice of God, is represented as causing, through the terror which it inspires, the hinds in their pregnant state prematurely to drop their young; although, according to our ideas of poetical imagery, this may not accord so well with the other images in the passage nor appear so beautiful and sublime as the image of the oaks trembling at the voice of Jehovah.

<!-- PAGE 1234 -->
<span style="color:#800000">fta580</span> “Etant que touche la gloire de Dieu.” — *Fr.* <span style="color:#800000">fta581</span> “Pour le craindre et servir comme il appartient.” — *Fr.* <span style="color:#800000">fta582</span> “Par le deluge.” — *Fr.* This is the view taken of the passage by the ancient versions. “God,” says the Chaldee, “in the generation of the deluge sat in judgment.” The Septuagint reads, “God shall make the deluge to be inhabited,” or “make the world habitable after it”; the Syriac, “God called back the deluge;” and the Arabic, “God restrained the deluge.” Ainsworth reads, “Jehovah sat at the flood,” and explains it as meaning “Noah’s flood.”

<span style="color:#800000">fta583</span> “Que c’est luy seul qui gouverne toutes choses en tout temps.” — *Fr.* <span style="color:#800000">fta584</span> “Se recognoissans estrangers, et que c’estoit luy qui les y logeoit et leur bailloit demeurance.” — *Fr*.

<span style="color:#800000">fta585</span> “Quand l’homme allegoit qu’il n’avoit encores dedid sa maison.” — *Fr*.

<span style="color:#800000">fta586</span> Ainsworth reads, “Thou hast drawn up me,” which he explains to mean, “drawn as out of a pit of waters;” “for,” says he, “this word is used for ‘drawing of waters,’ Exodus 2:16; waters signifying troubles.” “<span style="color:#008080">ybtyld</span>, *Thou hast drawn me up as it were out of a dungeon.” — Rogers’ Book of-Psalms.* <span style="color:#800000">fta587</span> “D’entre ceux que descendent.” — *Fr*.

<span style="color:#800000">fta588</span> “Ou chantez afin qu’il soit memoire.” — Fr. *marg.* “Or sing, that he may be remembered.”

<span style="color:#800000">fta589</span> Literally, “There is but a moment in his anger;” and this is also the literal rendering of the Hebrew.

<span style="color:#800000">fta590</span> “C’est, un long temps.” *— Note, Fr. marg.* “That is, a long time.”

<span style="color:#800000">fta591</span> <span style="color:#008080">rkzl</span>*, lezeker, at the memorial.* <span style="color:#800000">fta592</span> “Et de faict, c’est un poinct tout resolu.” — Fr.

<span style="color:#800000">fta593</span> “C’est, en ma prosperite.” *— Note, Fr. marg.* “That is, in my prosperity.”

<span style="color:#800000">fta594</span> Our author here uses *Dominus;* but in the Hebrew it is <span style="color:#008080">hwhy</span>, *Yehovah* <span style="color:#800000">fta595</span> The Septuagint has “<span style="color:#0000d4">Eijv διαθοράν</span>” — to corruption. The rendering of Jerome is the same, “In corruptionem.”

<!-- PAGE 1235 -->
<span style="color:#800000">fta596</span> “Qu’ils se mignardent en leur prosperite, et par maniere de dire, croupessent sur leur fumier. *— Fr.* <span style="color:#800000">fta597</span> This custom was not confined to the Israelites. It was practiced also among the heathen nations. An instance of this is recorded in Jonah 3:5-8. It appears from Plutarch, that this was also sometimes practiced among the Greeks. The Hebrew word for *sackcloth* is <span style="color:#008080">qç</span>, *sak;* and it is remarkable that the word *sak* exists in various languages, denoting the same thing. It shows the unaffected character of real sorrow, leading men to neglect the adorning of their persons, when we find several nations manifesting it by wearing the same dismal garb, and employing a word of the same sound to express it.

<span style="color:#800000">fta598</span> “Ne monstrant qu’abjection et desplaisanee d’eux-mesmes. *— Fr.* <span style="color:#800000">fta599</span> “Ou, adresse moy et conduy.” — *Fr*. *marg.* “Or, do thou direct and guide me.”

<span style="color:#800000">fta600</span> “Feroit une antithese entre ces deux choses, Estre en destresse pour un temps, et demeurer confus.” — *Fr*.

<span style="color:#800000">fta601</span> “Si los hommes regardent a leur dignite.” — *Fr*.

<span style="color:#800000">fta602</span> Dr Geddes observes, that this is the literal translation of the Hebrew words; “but,” says he, “as negative propositions in Hebrew are often equivalent in sense to opposite positives, I deemed it better to use an equivalent as more agreeable to what precerdes and follows.” His rendering is, “Rescued me from the hand of mine enemy.”

<span style="color:#800000">fta603</span> “Se faisans a croire que de leur faict ce ne sera que triomphe.” — *Fr*.

<span style="color:#800000">fta604</span> Horsley, while his translation is similar to that of Calvin, “Thou hast delivered me,” takes a somewhat different view of the meaning. “Thou hast, i.e., Thou most surely wilt. — The thing is as certain as if it were done.”

<!-- PAGE 1236 -->
<span style="color:#800000">fta605</span> Hammond considers “vanities” as referring to the practice of superstitiously having recourse to auguries and divinations for advice and direction, a practice which prevailed among the heathen, when they met with any difficulty or danger. To the responses of augury, they showed the greatest regard; although they were deceived and disappointed in the confidence which they reposed in them. David declares that he detested all such practices, and trusted for aid to God alone. French and Skinner, by *lying vanities,* understand *idols.* “Idols,” says Walford, “are often thus denominated; though the term is not to be confined to this sense, as all the pursuits of iniquity may be justly comprehended under it. - Vide Deuteronomy 32:21; Jonah 2:8.”

<span style="color:#800000">fta606</span> “There is a contrast in the expression between the straits to which he had been confined, and the freedom which was now bestowed upon him.” *— Walford.* <span style="color:#800000">fta607</span> “Ou, des grans.” — *Fr*. *marg.* “Or, of the great.”

<span style="color:#800000">fta608</span> “The word <span style="color:#008080">˚w[</span>,” says Hammond, “as it signifies *sin,* so it signifies also the punishment of sin, Isaiah 53:6, 11;” and in this last sense this critic here understands it, that it may be connected with *grief* and *sighing,* which are mentioned in the preceding clause, and may express those miseries which David’s sins had brought upon him. “<span style="color:#008080">[zw</span>” observes Rogers, “signifies here and in some other places, *affliction,* the punishment or consequence of sin; see Genesis 4:13; 1 Samuel 28:10; 2 Kings 7:9,” etc. - *Book of Psalms in Hebrew, metrically arranged,* vol. 2:p.188. The Septuagint reads, *in poverty* or *affliction, in* which it is followed by the Syriac and Vulgate.

<span style="color:#800000">fta609</span> “I am become like a broken vessel;” that is, utterly neglected as being worthless.

<span style="color:#800000">fta610</span> Horsley takes the same view. He reads, “the mighty.”

<span style="color:#800000">fta611</span> “*Fearfulness on every side,* or *terror round about.* In Heb., *magor missabib,* which name Jeremiah gave to Pashur the priest, signifying that he should be a terror to himself and to all his friends; Jeremiah 20:3, 4.” — *Ainsworth.* Horsley reads,

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">*“Truly I heard the angry muttering of the mighty,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*of them that are the general dread.”*</span></p>

On this he has the following note:“<span style="color:#008080">rwgm bybsm</span>, I take this to be a phrase describing the mighty, whose malignant threats against him he overheard, as persons universally dreaded for their power and their cruelty.”

<span style="color:#800000">fta612</span> “C’est, reservee.” — *Fr*. *marg.* “That is, laid up.”

<span style="color:#800000">fta613</span> “C’est a dire, a cause de leur adoption.” — *Fr*.

<!-- PAGE 1237 -->

<span style="color:#800000">fta614</span> *“Before the sons of men, i.e.* openly, so that the world must acknowledge ‘there is a reward for the righteous man.’ Compare Psalm 58:11.” *— French and Skinner.* <span style="color:#800000">fta615</span> “Et que quand elle luit sur les fideles, ses rayons sont pour esblouir les yeux de tous les iniques, et affoiblir leur mains.”. — *Fr.* <span style="color:#800000">fta616</span> This is the reading adopted by Walford. “<span style="color:#008080">yskrm, skr</span>, *colligavit:*hence ‘bands,’ ‘conspiracies.’”

<span style="color:#800000">fta617</span> “The particle of similitude is wanting in Hebrew, as is not uncommon. The intention of the Psalmist is evidently to describe by a metaphor his signal deliverance, as if he had been guarded by invincible fortifications.” — *Walford.* <span style="color:#800000">fta618</span> “Ou, perturbation; ou, hastivete.” — *Fr*. *marg.* “Or, perturbation; or, haste.”

<span style="color:#800000">fta619</span>”Ou, par excellence.” — “Or, excellently.” — *Fr. marg.* <span style="color:#800000">fta620</span> “Et recognoistre qu’en icelle ils ont assez de secours.” — *Fr*.

<span style="color:#800000">fta621</span> Literally, “with plenty.”

<span style="color:#800000">fta622</span> The word <span style="color:#008080">hag</span>, *gaäh,* from which <span style="color:#008080">hwag</span>, *gaävah,* which we have rendered *proudly,* is derived, signifies *elatus est, eminuit;* and <span style="color:#008080">hwag</span>, *gaävah,* “is sometimes taken in a bad sense for *pride* or *arrogance,* as in Psalm 10:2; and sometimes in a good sense for *splendor, magnificence, strength, excellence.* In the latter sense it is used of God, Psalm 68:34, *His height,* or *excellence and strength, are in the* *clouds.” — Hammond.* <span style="color:#800000">fta623</span> “Pour lequel nous avons traduit, Donnant instruction.” — *Fr*.

<span style="color:#800000">fta624</span> Where it is said, “I will instruct thee and teach thee.”

<span style="color:#800000">fta625</span> The translation of this verse in our English Bible is, “When I kept silence, my bones waxed old through my roaring all the day long;” on which Street observes, “I must own I do not understand how a man can be said *to keep silence* who *roars* all the day long.” Accordingly, instead of *When I kept silence,* he reads, *While I am lost in thought;* observing that, the verb <span style="color:#008080">çrj</span>, in the Hiphil conjugation, signifies *to ponder, to consider, to be deep in thought.”* But according to the translation and exposition of Calvin, there is no inconsistency between

<!-- PAGE 1238 -->

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">the first and the second clause of the verse. To avoid the apparent contradiction of being at once *silent* and yet *roaring* all the day long, Dr Boothroyd, instead of *roaring,* reads *pangs.*</p>

<span style="color:#800000">fta626</span> “Ou, peine.” — *Fr. marg.* “Or, punishment.”

<span style="color:#800000">fta627</span> “De grandes eaux.” — *Fr*. “Of great waters.”

<span style="color:#800000">fta628</span> In the Septuagint version it is rendered, “In the time of finding favor;” in the Arabic, “In a time of hearing;” and in the Syriac, “In an acceptable time.”

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#800000">fta629</span> “Qu’ils nous faut avoir des advocats au ciel qui prient pour nous.” — *Fr*.</p>

<span style="color:#800000">fta630</span> “Ou, je guideray de mon oeil.” — *Fr*. *marg.* “Or, I will guide thee with mine eye.”

<span style="color:#800000">fta631</span> This verse in the Hebrew is very elliptical and obscure. Hence, besides the translation of Calvin, which agrees very well with the scope of the passage, various other translations have been given of it. In our English Bible, the last clause is rendered, “lest they come near unto thee,” that is, to attack thee. But this is evidently an incorrect translation. This is not the common practice of these animals, which are timid, and not ferocious; bits and bridles are not used for the purpose of keeping them away from us, but of subduing, guiding, and making them subservient to our will; and were this the sense, the figure would be inappropriate, since the object of the Psalmist is to induce men to approach God. The clause, therefore, is rendered by many critics, “Or they will not come nigh unto thee;” that is, they will flee from thee. The Hebrew for this last phrase is, “There is not a coming to thee.”

<!-- PAGE 1239 -->
<span style="color:#800000">fta632</span> Most commentators consider Jehovah as the person speaking in this verse. Calvin, however, views David as the speaker. In this opinion he is followed by Walford. “In Psalm 51:13,” says this critic, “written about the same time and on the same occasion, David urges as a reason why God should restore to him the joy of his salvation, that he might be enabled to teach transgressors his ways, and that sinners might be converted to him. So in the passage before us, he addresses himself to sinners, and says, ‘I will instruct time, and teach thee the way in which thou shalt go.’“ <span style="color:#800000">fta633</span> Fry reads, “Many are the wounds of the refractory;” on which he has the following note:- “We perceive in this place the exact idea of <span style="color:#008080">jfb</span>, in its allusion to the restive, disobedient, unyielding, ungovernable mule or horse. It is opposed to ,, *to confide in, to yield to,* or *succumb,* as the gentle beast fully confides and yields himself to the management of his guide.”

<p class="title-block-h2" style="text-align:center; font-size:16px; font-weight:bold; margin:18px 0 12px;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#006411">PSALM 33</span></p>

<span style="color:#800000">fta634</span> “Ou, digne d’estre aimee par les,” etc. — *Fr*. *marg.* “Or, is worthy of being loved by them.”

<span style="color:#800000">fta635</span> “Fideles, c’est, fermes et permanentes.” — *Fr*. *marg.* “Faithful, that is, firm and permanent.”

<span style="color:#800000">fta636</span> “Et neant moins nous voyons ce que Sainct Paul en determine.” — *Fr*.

<span style="color:#800000">fta637</span> “C’est, le soufie, le vent.” *— Note, Fr. marg.* “That is, the breath.”

<span style="color:#800000">fta638</span> “Par son simple vouloir et commandement.” — *Fr.* “Simply by his will and commandment.”

<span style="color:#800000">fta639</span> “Sans aussi y employer beaucoup de temps ou travail.” — *Fr*.

<span style="color:#800000">fta640</span> In Genesis 1:9 we read, “God said, Let the waters under the heavens be gathered together into one place, and let the dry land appear:and it was so.” The Psalmist here probably has a reference to that passage, as in the 9th verse there is evidently an imitation of the style in which God is described in the first chapter of Genesis as performing the work of creation.

<span style="color:#800000">fta641</span> “Il a commande que si tost qu’il auroit comme prononce le mot, la chose aussi se trouvast faire.” — *Fr*.

<!-- PAGE 1240 -->
<span style="color:#800000">fta642</span> The Septuagint here adds a sentence which is not in the Hebrew, namely, “<span style="color:#0000d4">Καὶ ἀθετεῖ βουλὰς ἀρχόντων</span>”- “And frustrates the counsels of princes.” The Vulgate, Arabic, and Ethiopic, copying from the Septuagint, have the same addition. But it is not in the Chaldee and Syriac versions, which agree with our Hebrew Bibles; and from this we are led to conclude that the translators of the Greek version have added this by way of paraphrase; a liberty which we find them using in other places.

<span style="color:#800000">fta643</span> Hebrews *To generation and generation.* <span style="color:#800000">fta644</span> “C’est, sans en excepter un.” *— Note, Fr. marg,* “That is, without a single exception.”

<span style="color:#800000">fta645</span> “Hebrews est mensonge a salut.” — *Fr*. *marg.* “Hebrews is a lie for safety.”

<span style="color:#800000">fta646</span> “Ou, certes.” — *Fr*. *marg.* “Or, certainly.”

<p class="title-block-h2" style="text-align:center; font-size:16px; font-weight:bold; margin:18px 0 12px;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#006411">PSALM 34</span></p>

<span style="color:#800000">fta647</span> It should be 1 Samuel 21:11, 12.

<span style="color:#800000">fta648</span> Achish may have been his particular name, while Abimelech was the common title of the Kings of Gath. The word *Abimelech* signifies *Father — King.* <span style="color:#800000">fta649</span> Ainsworth reads, *His behavior,* or *his sense, reason;* and observes, that it is “properly *the taste,* as in verse 9, Job 6:6, and often elsewhere, which is used both for one’s in ward sense or reason, and outward gesture and demeanour, (as the Gr. here translateth it, *face,)* because by it a man is discerned and judged to be wise or foolish, as meats are discerned by the taste.”

<span style="color:#800000">fta650</span> “Luy melt aussi au coeur ceste finesse.” — *Fr*.

<span style="color:#800000">fta651</span> “That is, in all circumstances; in every posture of my affairs.” — *Horsley.* <span style="color:#800000">fta652</span> “Quand il ne cesse de nous bien-faire?” *— Fr.* “When he never ceases from doing us good ?”

<span style="color:#800000">fta653</span> The word <span style="color:#008080">µywn[</span>, *anavim,* may also be rendered *the afflicted.* Our author in his exposition combines both the ideas of *humble* and *afflicted.* <span style="color:#800000">fta654</span> “Et qu’il avoit bien occasion de penser que la cruaute d’iceluy ne se pourroit pas appaiser a le faire mourir de quelque legere mort.” — *Fr*.

<!-- PAGE 1241 -->
<span style="color:#800000">fta655</span> Those who take this view explain the words as meaning that the humble or afflicted, upon looking to David, saw how graciously God had dealt with him, and were enlightened, revived, and encouraged. They also consider, as Calvin himself does, the humble or afflicted as the persons who speak in the sixth verse, where, pointing as it were with the finger to David, they say, “This poor man cried,” etc.

<span style="color:#800000">fta656</span> This is the rendering adopted by Horsley, who understands by the expression the illumination of the soul by the light of Divine truth. He reads the verb in the imperative mood, and his translation of the entire verse is as follows:“Look towards him, and thou shalt be enlightened; And your faces shall never be ashamed.” This reading is sanctioned by the Septuagint. It supposes two alterations on the text. First, that instead of <span style="color:#008080">wfybh</span>*, they looked,* we should read <span style="color:#008080">wfybh</span>, *habitu, look ye;* and this last reading is supported by several of Dr Kennicott’s and De Rossi’s MSS. The other alteration is, that instead of <span style="color:#008080">çhynpw</span>, *upeneyhem, their faces,* we should read <span style="color:#008080">çkynpw</span>*, upeneykem, your faces.* Poole, in defense of reading *your* instead of *their,* observes, “that the change of persons is very frequent in this book.”

<span style="color:#800000">fta657</span> This description seems to have been suggested by Jacob’s vision of angels, recorded in Genesis 32:1, 2, “And Jacob went on his way, and the angels of God met him. And when Jacob saw them he said, This is God’s host; and he called the name of that place Mahanaim,” *(i.e., encampments.)* <span style="color:#800000">fta658</span> “Toute sorte de forteresse et lieu de defense.” *— Fr.* <span style="color:#800000">fta659</span> “Et ne s’avent plus de quel cost a se tourner.” — *Fr*.

<span style="color:#800000">fta660</span> By this affectionate appellation, Hebrew teachers were wont to address their scholars.

<span style="color:#800000">fta661</span> That is, the anger of God. The *face* of God is often put for the *anger* of God, because this passion manifests itself particularly in the face. Thus in Lamentations 4:16, we read, “The anger [literally *faces*] of the Lord hath divided them.” And in Leviticus 20:3, we read, “I will set my face [that is, *my anger*] against that man.”

<span style="color:#800000">fta662</span> In his First Epistle, (1 Peter 3:10, 11, 12,) he quotes the 12, 13, 14, 15, and 16 verses of this psalm. He quotes from the Septuagint.

<!-- PAGE 1242 -->

<span style="color:#800000">fta663</span> “Lesquels il espargne pour un temps, afin de les ruiner eternellement.” – “Whom he spares for a time, to destroy them eternally.” – *Fr.* <span style="color:#800000">fta664</span> It is wicked men who are spoken of in the immediately preceding verse; but *they* here evidently refers not to them, but to the righteous, mentioned in the fifteenth verse; and, accordingly, in all the ancient versions, and in our English Bible, the words *the righteous* are supplied. It is supposed by those who make this supplement, that the word <span style="color:#008080">µyqydx</span>, *tsaddikim,* has been lost out of the text. But if we read the 16th verse as a parenthesis, it will not be necessary to make any supplement, and the words may be read exactly as they are in the Hebrew, *They cried.* <span style="color:#800000">fta665</span> The last clause of this verse is applied in the Gospel of John (John 19:36) to Christ, and represented as receiving its fulfillment in him.

<p class="title-block-h2" style="text-align:center; font-size:16px; font-weight:bold; margin:18px 0 12px;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#006411">PSALM 35</span></p>

<span style="color:#800000">fta666</span> The word rendered *shield* is in the Hebrew text <span style="color:#008080">˚gm</span>, *magen,* which was a short buckler intended merely for defense. The word rendered *buckler* is <span style="color:#008080">hnx</span>, *tsinnah,* for an account of which see note, p. 64. The *tsinnah* was double the weight of the magen, and was carried by the infantry; the magen, being lighter and more manageable, was used by the cavalry. The *tsinnah* answered to the *scutum,* and the *magen* to the *clypeus,* among the Romans. — See *Paxton’s Illustrations of Scripture,* vol. 3:pp. 866, 867.

<span style="color:#800000">fta667</span> Those who are of opinion that <span style="color:#008080">rwgs</span>, *segor,* is a noun, translate it “the scymitar,” and read, “Draw out the spear, and the scymitar, to oppose my persecutors.” According to Drusius, Vitringa, Michaelis, Dr Kennicott, and others, the word means <span style="color:#0000d4">sagariv</span>, or scymitar, a sort of battle-axe, which was used by the Persians, Scythians, and other nations in ancient times.

<!-- PAGE 1243 -->
<span style="color:#800000">fta668</span> “Que l’esperance de mon salut surpasse tous les dangers qui me seront livrez. “*— Fr.* <span style="color:#800000">fta669</span> “C’est, chasse et presse.” *— Note, Fr. marg.* “That is, chase and pursue them.”

<span style="color:#800000">fta670</span> The allusion here is to the custom of digging pits and putting nets in them, covered with straw, etc., to catch wild beasts.

<span style="color:#800000">fta671</span> “Qu’il fait yci contre Saul comme le chef, s’estend a tout le corps, c’est a dire, tous ses adherens.” — *Fr*.

<span style="color:#800000">fta672</span> “C’est, desconforter.” *Note, Fr. marg.* “That is, to the discomfort;”

<span style="color:#800000">fta673</span> “<span style="color:#008080">smj yd[</span> *witnesses of wrong or violence;* i.e., witnesses deponing to acts of violence, as committed by the person accused. See Psalm 27:12.” *— Horsley.* <span style="color:#800000">fta674</span> “Ont tasch, de rendre orpheline car il y a ainsi proprement en Hebrieu” — *Fr*.

<span style="color:#800000">fta675</span> “When the Orientals,” says Boothroyd, “pray seriously in grief, they hide their face in their bosom:and to this custom the Psalmist here alludes. Rabbi Levi, Dathe, and others, explain it in like manner.”

<span style="color:#800000">fta676</span> The word is derived from <span style="color:#008080">hkn</span>, *nakah, to strike* or *to smite.* The LXX. render it <span style="color:#0000d4">mastigev</span>, *scourges;* and Jerome reads *percutientes, smiters,* in which he is followed by Ainsworth, who understands the word as meaning smiters with the tongue, or calumniators, and who thinks that the LXX., in translating it *scourges,* alluded to the scourge of the tongue, as in Job 5:21; and if *smiters* is the proper rendering, we may certainly conclude, that as this smiting is represented as done upon the person who was its object in his absence, it was a smiting by the tongue. At the same time, this critic observes, that the word may be read *the smitten,* that is, *abjects, vile persons,* as in Job 30:8. Dr Kennicott translates it by *verbcrones, whipt slaves, vile scoundrels.* Another meaning of the word, according to Buxtorff, is, *the wry-legged, the lame.* In this sense it is used in 2 Samuel 4:4, and 9:3; and hence the epithet of *Necho* was given to one of the Pharaohs, who halted in his gait. Thus it easily came to be employed as a term of contempt. Calvin and the translators of our English Bible agree in the meaning which they attach to this word.

<!-- PAGE 1244 -->

<span style="color:#800000">fta677</span> The verb <span style="color:#008080">[rq</span>, *kara,* for *cut,* “is significant of tearing or rending, and by an easy metaphor, is applicable to wounds inflicted by evil speaking and slander.” — *Walford.* <span style="color:#800000">fta678</span> Domine — *Lat*. <span style="color:#008080">ynda</span>, Adonai*. — Hebrews* “More than fifteen copies collected by Dr Kennicott have <span style="color:#008080">hwhy</span> here instead of <span style="color:#008080">ynda</span> Among which is one of the best manuscripts that has been collated. The Jews, in later ages, had a superstitious fear of pronouncing the word <span style="color:#008080">hwhy</span> and therefore inserted <span style="color:#008080">ynda</span> or <span style="color:#008080">µyhla</span> ,c’est a dire in the place of it very frequently.” *— Street.* <span style="color:#800000">fta679</span> “Assavoir, mon ame unique; c’est a dire, solitaire et delaissee.” — *Note, Fr. marg.* “Namely, my soul alone; that is to say, solitary and forsaken.” See note 3, p. 432. In our English Bible the phrase is, “My darling;” but David rather means to intimate his forsaken and unprotected condition, unless God interposed in his behalf. Green reads, “my helpless person.”

<span style="color:#800000">fta680</span> “Devant un grand peuple.” — *Fr*.

<span style="color:#800000">fta681</span> “C’est, beaucoup de peuple.” — *Note, Fr. marg.* “That is, much people.”

<span style="color:#800000">fta682</span> Horsley takes this view. He reads, “*Among a mighty people*;” and observes, that this is the rendering of the Chaldee, and that <span style="color:#008080">µx[</span>, seems more properly to express strength or power than number.

<span style="color:#800000">fta683</span> “C’est, ne tienent propos d’amis.” *— Note, Fr. marg.* “That is, their discourse is not that of friends.”

<span style="color:#800000">fta684</span> “C’est, ce que nous desirions.” *— Note, Fr. marg.* “That is, that which we desire.” French and Skinner read, “Aha! aha! our eye seeth!” “that is,” they observe, “beholds our enemy in the fallen condition in which we desire to see him. See verse 25, compare Psalm 92:11.”

<!-- PAGE 1245 -->
<span style="color:#800000">fta685</span> “C’est, nostre desire nous avons ce que desirions:ou, nostre ame, assavoir s’esjouisse, comme on dit en nostre langue, Grande chere.” — *Note, Fr. marg.* “That is, our desire — we have what we desire:or, our soul, that is to say, is glad:as we say in our language, Great cheer.” French and Skinner read, “Let them not say in their hearts, Aha! our desire!” and observe, “that *our desire!* means our desire is accomplished.”

<p class="title-block-h2" style="text-align:center; font-size:16px; font-weight:bold; margin:18px 0 12px;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#006411">PSALM 36</span></p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftb1</span> “C’est, tant que chacun commence a avoir en haine l’iniquite d’iceluy.” — *Fr*. *marg*. “That is, so that every one begins to hate his iniquity.”

<span style="color:#800000">ftb2</span> “Mensonge.” — *Fr*. “Falsehood.”

<span style="color:#800000">ftb3</span> The verb <span style="color:#008080">ãlj</span>, *chalak*, which is rendered *flattereth*, signifies *to smooth*, and means here, that the wicked man described endeavors by plausible arguments to put a soft, smooth, and fair gloss on his wickedness, as if there were nothing repulsive and hateful about it, nothing amiss or blame-worthy in it; and in this way he deceives himself. This is the sense expressed in the literal translation of Montanus, which seems very forcible: “Quoniam lenivit ad se in oculis ipsius, ad inveniendum iniquitatem suam ad odiendam.” — “For he has smoothed over [or set a πολισή to himself in his own eyes, with respect to the finding out of his iniquity, [that is, so as not to find it out,] to hate it.” Horsley reads,

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">*“For he giveth things a fair appearance to himself,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*In his own eyes, so that he discovers not his own*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*iniquityto hate it.”*</span></p>

“He sets such a false gloss, “ says this critic, “in his own eyes, upon his worst actions, that he never finds out the blackness of his iniquity, which, were it perceived by him, would be hateful even to himself.” The wicked in all ages have thus contrived to put a fair appearance upon the most unprincipled maxims and pernicious practices. It will be seen that Montanus’ and Horsley’s translation of the last clause of the verse gives a different meaning from that given by Calvin. The original text is somewhat obscure and ambiguous from its brevity; but it seems to support the sense given by these critics. The Hebrew is, <span style="color:#008080">ançlwnw[</span> <span style="color:#008080">axml</span>, *limtso avono lisno*, *to find*, or *to*, *for*, or *concerning the finding of*, [the first word being an infinitive with the prefix <span style="color:#008080">l</span>, *lamed*,] *his iniquity to hate* [*it*.] “The prefix <span style="color:#008080">l</span>,” says Walford, “cannot, I imagine, be translated with any propriety by *until*.” His rendering is,

<!-- PAGE 1246 -->

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">*“For he flattereth himself in his own sight,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*That his iniquity will not be found to be hateful:”*</span></p>

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">That is, will not be viewed by others as the hateful thing which it really is. The original words will easily bear this sense as well as that given by Montanus and Horsley.</p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftb4</span> In the French version it is, “Comme hautes montagnes;” — “as the high mountains;” and in the margin Calvin states that the Hebrew is, “Montagnes de Dieu;” — “Mountains of God.” The Hebrews were accustomed to describe things eminent, as Calvin observes in his exposition of the verse, by adding to them the name of God; as, “river of God”; Psalm 65:9; “mount of God,” Psalm 68:15; “cedars of God,” Psalm 80:10; “the trees of the Lord,” <span style="color:#800000"><19A416></span>Psalm 104:16. “The mountains of God,” therefore, here mean *the highest mountain*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftb5</span> Lowth reads, “A vast abyss.”

<span style="color:#800000">ftb6</span> Heb. — how precious.

<span style="color:#800000">ftb7</span> “En toy.” — *Fr*. “In thee.”

<span style="color:#800000">ftb8</span> “Par ta clarte.” — *Fr*. “By thy light.”

<span style="color:#800000">ftb9</span> “Frequens in Psalmis figura ab alio Cherubinorum Arcae,” etc. i.e. “A common figure in the Psalms, taken more immediately, in my opinion, from the wings of the Cherubim overshadowing the mercy-seat which covered the ark; but more remotely from birds, which defend their young from the solar rays by overshadowing them with their wings. See Psalm 17:8; 57:1; 61:4; 91:4, etc., and Deuteronomy 32:11.” — *Bishop Hare*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftb10</span> The words in the original are, <span style="color:#008080">˚yt[ ljn</span>, *nachal adanecha*, *the river of thy Eden*, in which there is probably an allusion to the garden of <span style="color:#008080">ˆr[</span> Eden, and to the river which flowed through and watered it.

<span style="color:#800000">ftb11</span> Heb. Draw out at length.

<!-- PAGE 1247 -->
<span style="color:#800000">ftb12</span> That is, the foot of the proud man, as the Chaldee translates it, the thing being put for the person in whom it is; a mode of expression of frequent occurrence in Scripture. Thus *deceit*, in Proverbs 12:27, is put for *a deceitful man*; *poverty*, in 2 Kings 24:14, *for poor people*, etc. There appears to be here an allusion to the ancient practice of tyrants in treading upon their enemies, or in spurning those who offended them from their presence with their feet.

<span style="color:#800000">ftb13</span> Heb. <span style="color:#008080">µç</span> *sham*, *there*, that is, (pointing with the finger to a particular place,) see there! lo! the workers of iniquity are fallen. “It represents strongly before the eye,” says Mudge, “the downfall of the wicked. Upon the very spot where they practice their treachery, they receive their downfall.” A similar mode of expression occurs in Psalm 14:5.

<p class="title-block-h2" style="text-align:center; font-size:16px; font-weight:bold; margin:18px 0 12px;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#006411">PSALM 37</span></p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftb14</span> “C’est, jouy des biens d’icelle en repos ferme et asseure.” — *Fr*. *marg*. “That is, enjoy the good things of it in quietness and security.”

<span style="color:#800000">ftb15</span> “C’est, ton bon droict.” — *Fr*. *marg*. “That is, thy just cause, or thy rectitude.

<span style="color:#800000">ftb16</span> That is, do not enter into fellowship with.

<span style="color:#800000">ftb17</span> The fitness of this figure to express the transient and short-lived character of the prosperity of the wicked, will appear in a still more striking light when we take into consideration the great heat of the climate of Palestine.

<span style="color:#800000">ftb18</span> Some read, “Thou shalt dwell in the land.” The Hebrew verb is in the imperative mood; but the imperative in Hebrew is sometimes used for the future of the indicative. — Glass. tom. 1, can. 40, p. 285.

<span style="color:#800000">ftb19</span>”C’est dire, qui te vient loyaument.” — *Fr*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftb20</span> Modern critics have varied as much in their interpretations of this clause of the verse as those who preceded Calvin, of whom he complains. For example, Ainsworth reads, “Thou shalt be fed by faith;” Archbishop Secker,” Thou shalt be fed in plenty;” Parkhurst, “Thou shalt be fed in security;” Dathe, “Tunc terram inhabitabis et secure vivas,” assigning the reason for this translation to be, that “*pascere securitatem*, sive si malis, *in securitate*, nihil aliud est quam *secure vivere*;” and Gesenius reads, “Follow after truth,” or, “seek to be faithful,” deriving the verb from a root which signifies *to take delight in*, or *to follow after*.

<!-- PAGE 1248 -->

<span style="color:#800000">ftb21</span> “D’autant que Dieu est la part de nostre heritage, que nostre lot est escheu en lieux plaisan,.” — *Fr*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftb22</span>Calvin here gives the exact sense of the Hebrew verb <span style="color:#008080">llg</span>, *galal*. It literally signifies *to roll*, or *to devolve*; and in this passage it evidently means, Roll or devolve all thy concerns upon God; “cast thy burden upon him,” as it is in Psalm 55:22; “the metaphor being taken,” says Cresswell, “from a burden put by one who is unequal to it upon a stronger man.” But Dr Adam Clarke thinks that the idea may be taken from the camel who lies down till his load be rolled upon him.

<span style="color:#800000">ftb23</span> “Ou, qui vient a bont de ses entreprises.” — *Fr*. *marg*. “Or, who accomplishes his devices.”

<span style="color:#800000">ftb24</span> “C’est, y auront leurs plaisirs avec grande prosperite.” — *Fr*. *marg*. “That is, shall have their enjoyment in it with great prosperity.”

<span style="color:#800000">ftb25</span> The Hebrew verb rendered *silent* is <span style="color:#008080">µwd</span>, *dom*, from which the English word *dumb* appears to be derived. The silence here enjoined is opposed to murmuring or complaining. The word is rendered by the Septuagint, <span style="color:#0000d4">uJpotagnqi</span>, *be subject*; which is not an exact translation of the original term: but it well expresses the meaning; for this silence implies the entire subjection of ourselves to the will of God.

<span style="color:#800000">ftb26</span> “De se venger, et de rendre mal pour mal.” — *Fr*. “To take revenge, and to render evil for evil.”

<span style="color:#800000">ftb27</span> Dominus. Heb. <span style="color:#008080">yta</span>, Adonai.

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1"><span style="color:#800000">ftb28</span> “Comme s’ils avoyent puissance de faire de nous a leur plaisir.” — *Fr*.</p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftb29</span> “Day is often used”, says Ainsworth, “for the time of punishment; as, ‘the posterity shall be astonied at his day,’ Job 18:20; ‘Woe unto them, for their day is come!’ Jeremiah 1:27. So ‘the day of Midian,’ Isaiah 9:4; ‘the day of Jezreel,’ Hosea 1:11; ‘the day of Jerusalem,’ Psalm 137: 7.”

<span style="color:#800000">ftb30</span> “De brebis destinees au sacrifice.” — *Fr*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftb31</span> “Ou, aux grans qui sont meschans.” — *Fr*. *marg*. “Or, to the great who are wicked.”

<!-- PAGE 1249 -->
<span style="color:#800000">ftb32</span> Ainsworth renders this word, “plenteous mammon,” which, he remarks, “signifieth multitude, plenty, or store of riches, or any other thing.” The Septuagint renders it *riches*. The English word *mammon* is derived from this Hebrew word.

<span style="color:#800000">ftb33</span> This is the view taken by Fry, who renders the words,

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">*“Better are the few of the JUST ONE,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Than the great multitude of the wicked.”*</span></p>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1">By the JUST ONE, he understands Christ.</p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftb34</span> “‘Depositeth the days of the upright,” — lays them up in safety for them: for such is the original idea of <span style="color:#008080">[ry</span>.” — *Fry*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftb35</span> “Ou, l’excellence, c’est, les agneaux plus beaux et plus gras.” — *Fr*. *marg*. “Or, the excellency, that is, the finest and fattest lambs.”

<span style="color:#800000">ftb36</span> “C’est, s’esvanouiront en brief.” — *Fr*. *marg*. “That is, shall speedily vanish away.”

<span style="color:#800000">ftb37</span> It is generally supposed that there is here an allusion to the sacrificial services of the former dispensation. Lambs were then offered in large numbers as burnt-offerings; and if the allusion is to these sacrifices, as is highly probable, the doctrine taught is, that as the fat of them melted away, and was wholly and rapidly consumed by the fire of the altar of burnt-offering, so the wicked shall melt away and be quickly consumed in the fire of Jehovah’s wrath. The Chaldee paraphrases the last clause thus: — “They shall be consumed in the smoke of Gehenna,” or of hell.

<span style="color:#800000">ftb38</span> “Comme escumeurs de mer sans jamais avoir de quoy satisfaire.” — *Fr*. “Like pirates, without ever having any thing to pay.”

<span style="color:#800000">ftb39</span> “Comme s’il y avoit, Ceux qui beniront les justes, possederont,” etc. — *Fr*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftb40</span> “Neither the text,” says Dr Adam Clarke, “nor any of the versions, intimate that a falling into sin is meant; but a falling into *trouble*, *difficulty*,” etc.

<span style="color:#800000">ftb41</span> This is also the reading of the Septuagint, <span style="color:#0000d4">Τὸ σπέζμα aujtou eijv</span> <span style="color:#0000d4">εὐλογίαν e[stai</span>.

<span style="color:#800000">ftb42</span> Ainsworth reads, “And his seed are in the blessing,” and understands the words as meaning, that the children of the just man “are in the

<!-- PAGE 1250 -->

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">blessing, or are appointed to the blessing, as the heirs thereof,”</p>

Genesis 28:3; 1 Peter 3:9; and that they have still

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">abundance, notwithstanding the liberality of their parents; for “the blessing of the Lord maketh rich,” Proverbs 10:22.</p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftb43</span> “Par lesquelles ils taschent d’espouvanter les simples.” — *Fr*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftb44</span> “En toutes les parties de la cognoissance et crainte de Dieu.” — *Fr*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftb45</span> Striking terror in all around.

<span style="color:#800000">ftb46</span> The proper signification of the word <span style="color:#008080">jrza</span>, *azrach*, has been controverted among interpreters, and it has been variously rendered. Most of the Rabbins, and many modern commentators, as Mudge, Waterland, Gesenius, and others, are of opinion, that the preferable reading is, “like an indigenous or native tree;” that is, a tree which flourishes in its native soil, where it grows most vigorously, and acquires its largest and most luxuriant growth. The Septuagint translates it, <span style="color:#0000d4">ὼς τὰς χέδρους tou Λιβάνου</span>, “as the cedars of Lebanon;” being self-growing, spreading, and lofty trees. Some suppose that the translators of this version must have had a different reading in their Hebrew Bibles from what is in our present copies; and others that, as is common with them, they paraphrase the original words, the more clearly to express their meaning. The translation of the Septuagint is followed by the Vulgate, Arabic, and Ethiopic versions, by Houbigant, Boothroyd, Geddes, and other good authorities. Ainsworth reads, “as a green self-growing laurel.” Bythner says he is at a loss for the reason of translating the word *laurel*. “For the reading of *bay tree*,” says the illustrated Commentary upon the Bible, “we are not aware of any authority, except the very feeble one which is offered by some of the older of the modern versions in this country and on the Continent.”

<span style="color:#800000">ftb47</span> The Suptuagint, Vulgate, Syriac, and Arabic versions, Jerome, Houbigant, Horsley, and Walford, read the verb in the first person, “But I passed by.” The Chaldee adheres to the Hebrew, “And he passed, *or* failed, from the age, *or* world, and, lo! he was not.”

<!-- PAGE 1251 -->

<p class="title-block-h2" style="text-align:center; font-size:16px; font-weight:bold; margin:18px 0 12px;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#006411">PSALM 38</span></p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftb48</span> This title occurs only here and in the 70th psalm. This psalm is the third of what are called the Penitential Psalms. The two before this are the 6th and the 32d; and the four which follow it are the 51st, the 102d, the 130th, and the 143d. It is a curious fact, that when Galileo was sentenced to be confined in the dungeons of the Inquisition for an indefinite period, for having maintained the Copernican system, he was enjoined to repeat as a penance these seven Penitential Psalms every week for three years; by which it was doubtless intended to extort a sort of confession from him of his guilt, and an acknowledgement of the justice of his sentence.

<span style="color:#800000">ftb49</span> That is, they enter deep into the flesh. The Septuagint reads, “<span style="color:#0000d4">Ενεπάγησάν moi</span>” the Vulgate, “Infixae sunt mihi;” — “Are fastened in me;” which is a natural consequence of entering deep, and rather expresses the meaning, than conveys the precise idea of the original word. The Syriac and Arabic versions give the same rendering with the Vulgate.

<span style="color:#800000">ftb50</span> “The proper meaning of <span style="color:#008080">rkj</span> is not a wound, but a bruise or wale made by a severe blow. My wales through my severe chastisement are become putrid and running sores.” — *Fry*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftb51</span> Berlin reads, “aestu torrente;” Horsley, “with a parching heat;” and this is the view taken by Hare, Dathe, Gesenius, and the Chaldee.

<span style="color:#800000">ftb52</span> Dominus. In the Hebrew Bible it is <span style="color:#008080">ynda</span>, *Adonai*; but several MSS. read <span style="color:#008080">hahy</span>, *Yehovah*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftb53</span> “<span style="color:#008080">rdq</span> is literally ‘dressed in mourning;’ hence it may, by an easy figure, denote the melancholy looks of a mourner.” — *Horsley*. This is the sense put upon the expression by the Septuagint, “<span style="color:#0000d4">Olhn τὴν</span> <span style="color:#0000d4">hJmeron σχυθρωπάζ wn ἐπορευόμης</span>;” — “I went with a mourning countenance all the day”.

<span style="color:#800000">ftb54</span>”Et machine des finesses pour le surprendre.” — *Fr*. “And devised stratagems for ensnaring him.”

<span style="color:#800000">ftb55</span> “Comme celles des orateurs profanes.” — *Fr*.

<!-- PAGE 1252 -->

<span style="color:#800000">ftb56</span> Dominus. Heb. <span style="color:#008080">yta</span>, *Adonai*. But instead of <span style="color:#008080">yta</span>, *Adonai*, one hundred and two of Kennicott’s and De Rossi’s MSS. read <span style="color:#008080">hwhy</span>, *Yehovah*, which may be presumed to be the true reading. As the Jews, from the sacredness which they attach to the name *Jehovah*, never pronounce it, and when it occurs in reading the Scriptures, pronounce <span style="color:#008080">yta</span>, *Adonai*, it may readily be supposed that Jewish scribes, in writing out copies of the Scriptures, from their constantly reading *Adonai* for *Jehovah*, would be very apt to fall into the mistake of writing the former word for the latter.

<span style="color:#800000">ftb57</span> “Et que son affliction est telle, qu’il ne sera jour de sa vie qu’il ne s’en sente.” — *Fr*. “And that his affliction was such, that there would not be a day of his life but he would feel it.”

<span style="color:#800000">ftb58</span> Ainsworth reads, “*are alive*, or *living*;” “that is,” says he, “lively, lusty, cheerful, hale, and sound, or rich, as the word seemeth to mean in Ecclesiastes 6:8.” Dr Lowth, instead of <span style="color:#008080">µyyj</span>, *chayim*, *living*, proposes to read here <span style="color:#008080">µnaj</span>, *chinam*, *without cause* — *without cause have strengthened themselves*. “I think,” says he, “<span style="color:#008080">µnyj</span>, here for <span style="color:#008080">µyyj</span>, is a remarkable instance of a reading merely conjectural, unsupported by any authority but that of the context, of the truth of which, no possible doubt can be made. Hare and Houbigant, and I suppose every other competent reader, has hit upon it. You see the two hemistichs are parallel and synonymous, word answering to word.” — *Dr Lowth in Mr Merrick*’*s Note on this place*. — Street and Dr Adam Clarke agree in this alteration.

<span style="color:#800000">ftb59</span> Dominus. Heb. <span style="color:#008080">ynda</span>, Adonai.

<span style="color:#800000">ftb60</span> “Ou, de mon salut.” — *Fr*. *marg*. “Or, of my salvation.”

<p class="title-block-h2" style="text-align:center; font-size:16px; font-weight:bold; margin:18px 0 12px;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#006411">PSALM 39</span></p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftb61</span> The Hebrew word <span style="color:#008080">µwsjm</span>, *machsom*, rendered *bridle* in our English version, properly signifies a *muzzle*, and is so rendered in Deuteronomy 25:4. “Our translations,” observes Mant, “say

<!-- PAGE 1253 -->
‘as with a bridle.’ But we do not see how a bridle would preclude the person from speaking; nor is it a correct phrase, which the word muzzle is.” It is probable that the bridles of the ancients were made in the form of muzzles.

<span style="color:#800000">ftb62</span> Dr Geddes renders the last clause of the verse, “While the wicked prosper before me.”

<span style="color:#800000">ftb63</span> French and Skinner read, “I held my peace from good and bad.” In the Hebrew it is simply “from good;” but they observe, “This expression occurs frequently in Scripture, and it would seem, that owing to the constant use of it, one part only of the sentence has been here expressed. Thus, ‘Take heed that thou speak not to Jacob *either good or bad*,’ (Genesis 31:24.) Again, ‘Absalom spake *neither good nor bad*,’ (2 Samuel 13:22.”)

<span style="color:#800000">ftb64</span> Or, as Horsley reads, “how brief I am.”

<span style="color:#800000">ftb65</span> The word *riches* is a supplement; there being no word for it in Calvin’s version, nor in the Hebrew text; but the meaning evidently is, “they heap up, accumulate, or amass riches.” Horsley reads, “His accumulated riches — he knoweth not who shall gather them.”

<span style="color:#800000">ftb66</span> “*Mine age*, i.e., the whole extent of my life.” — *Cresswell*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftb67</span> The word <span style="color:#008080">lbh</span>, *hebel*, rendered *vanity*, according to some, means *the mirage*, that deceptive appearance of a collection of waters in the distance, which the traveler, through the Arabian deserts, imagines he sees before him, and from which he fondly hopes to quench his thirst; but which, upon his coming up to it, he finds to be only burning sands, to which the reflection of the light of the sun had given the appearance of a lake of water. According to others, vanity means *a vapor*, as the breath of one’s mouth, which speedily vanishes; to which the apostle refers in James 4:14. “I take the word in its proper sense,” [vapor,] says Bishop Mant, “as more poetical and energetic than the derivative one of ‘vanity.’” See Simonis and Parkhurst on <span style="color:#008080">lbh</span>. Abel gave to his second son the name of *Hebel*, *vanity*, and here David declares that <span style="color:#008080">µdaAlk</span> *col-adam*, all adam, every man is *hebel*, vanity.

<span style="color:#800000">ftb68</span> This word here rendered *standeth* “is well paraphrased by Dathe, ‘Dum firmissime constitutus videatur.’” — *Rogers*’ *Psalms in Heb*., *volume*2, p. 200.

<!-- PAGE 1254 -->

<span style="color:#800000">ftb69</span> In the Hebrew it is literally, “Man walketh in an image;” a phantasm, that which seems to be something real and substantial, but which does not deserve that character, which is an appearance only. Life is a mere show; “the baseless fabric of a vision;” it has the semblance of solidity, but there is no reality in it. The word occurs again in Psalm 73:20, “Thou shalt despise their image;” their vain show, or phantastic prosperity. Walford reads, “walketh *as* a shadow;” observing, that “the prefix <span style="color:#008080">b</span> is often used for <span style="color:#008080">k</span> as a particle of similitude.” he farther observes, that Dathe’s translation, “he pursues a shadow,” gives a good sense, but does not convey the exact notion of the figure that is conveyed by the Hebrew.

<span style="color:#800000">ftb70</span> “Et je ne scay quelle parade et ostentation.” — *Fr*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftb71</span> It is important to mark the difference between the Hebrew word <span style="color:#008080">rkx</span>, *tsabar*, here rendered *to heap together*, and the Word <span style="color:#008080">ãsa</span>, *asaph*, rendered *to gather*. “The former,” says Hammond, “here appears to contain all the toil of the harvest, in reaping, binding, setting up, and heaping things together, bringing them from the several places where they grow, into a *cumulus*. The latter denotes the stowing or housing, laying it up, removing or carrying it out of the field, where it is heaped or set up, ready for carriage. For so <span style="color:#008080">ãsa</span> signifies sometimes *to lay up*, sometimes *to take away*. This, then, is the description of the vanity of our human estate, that when a man hath run through all the labors of acquisition, and hath nothing visible to interpose betwixt him and his enjoyments, yet even then he is uncertain, not only whether himself shall possess it at last, but whether his heir shall do it; nay, he knows not whether his enemy may not; he cannot tell ‘who shall gather them into the barn,’ or enjoy them when they are there.”

<span style="color:#800000">ftb72</span> In the original it is <span style="color:#008080">ynda</span>; but in some MSS. it is <span style="color:#008080">hwhy</span>, which is probably the true reading.

<span style="color:#800000">ftb73</span> “Ou, vauneant et desbauche, ou, meschant.” — *Fr*. *marg*. “Or, the idle and debauched, or, wicked.”

<span style="color:#800000">ftb74</span> “Car il use d’un mot par lequel les Hebrieux signifient un homme vertueux, courageux, ou excellent.” — *Fr*. The Hebrew word is <span style="color:#008080">çya</span>, *ish*. See volume 1, p. 40, note.

<!-- PAGE 1255 -->

<span style="color:#800000">ftb75</span> The meaning according to our English version seems to be, that the beauty of man is consumed as the moth is consumed. “But,” says Walford, “this gives no correct or suitable sense. The design is to state, not that the moth is consumed, but that it is a consumer or spoiler of garments.” He reads,

<p style="text-align:center; color:#000080; margin:14px 2em;"><span style="color:#000080">*“With rebukes thou chastisest man for iniquity,*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*Then thou destroyest his goodliness as a moth*</span> <span style="color:#000080">*destroyeth a garment.”*</span></p>

This is precisely Calvin’s interpretation. The moth is called in Hebrew <span style="color:#008080">ç[</span>, *ash*, from its *corroding* and *destroying* the texture of cloth, etc. See Parkhurst’s Lexicon on the word <span style="color:#008080">ç[</span>. The metaphor here employed is of frequent occurrence in Scripture. For example, in Hosea 5:12, God says, “I will be to Ephraim as a moth,” that is,

I will consume them; and in Isaiah 50:9, it is said, “The moth shall eat them as a garment.”

<span style="color:#800000">ftb76</span> The original word, which Calvin renders “excellency,” is translated by Hammond “precious things;” by which he understands wealth, greatness, health, beauty, strength, and, in short, whatever is most precious to us.

<span style="color:#800000">ftb77</span> “ Ne dissimule point.” — *Fr*. “Dissemble not.”

<span style="color:#800000">ftb78</span> “Comme des gens qui sont logez en une maison par emprunt.” — *Fr*.

<p class="title-block-h2" style="text-align:center; font-size:16px; font-weight:bold; margin:18px 0 12px;" markdown="1"><span style="color:#006411">PSALM 40</span></p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftb79</span> “C’est, paciemment.” — *Fr*. *marg*. “That is, patiently.” Calvin in the text gives the literal rendering of the Hebrew. *In waiting I waited* is a Hebraism which signifies vehement desire, and yet entire resignation of mind. “The doubling of the word,” says Ainsworth, “denotes earnestness, constancy, patience.”

<span style="color:#800000">ftb80</span> The Septuagint reads, “<span style="color:#0000d4">Ec λάχχου ταλαιπωρίας</span>.” — “Out of a pit of misery;” and Ainsworth, “the pit of sounding calamity,” or “dungeon of tumultuous desolation, which,” says he, “echoed and resounded with dreadful noises.” “The sufferings of the Psalmist,” observes Bishop Mant, “are here described under the image of a dark subterraneous cavern from which there was no emerging; and where

<!-- PAGE 1256 -->

<p style="text-align:center" markdown="1">roaring cataracts of water broke in upon him, overwhelming him on every side, till, as it is expressed in the 18th psalm, ‘God sent from above and took him, and drew him out of many waters.’”</p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftb81</span> “Un marveilleux bruit.” — *Fr*. “A marvellous noise.”

<span style="color:#800000">ftb82</span> “A vanite.” — *Fr*. “To vanity.”

<span style="color:#800000">ftb83</span> “Devant toy.” — *Fr*. “Before thee, or in thy presence.”

<span style="color:#800000">ftb84</span> “Ou vanite” — *Fr*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftb85</span> “Sont grandes ou infinies.” — *Fr*. “Are great or innumerable.”

<span style="color:#800000">ftb86</span> “This verb,” says Ainsworth, “is sometimes used for matching or comparing.” In this sense the word occurs in Psalm 89:7; and this is the sense in which the Septuagint understands it here: “<span style="color:#0000d4">Καὶ</span> <span style="color:#0000d4">τοῖς διαλογισμοῖς sou oujc έστι tiv ὁμοιωθήσεται soi</span>;” — “and in thy thoughts there is none who shall be likened to thee.” Street reads, “There is none to be compared to thee;” and observes, that “above sixty copies of Dr Kennicott’s collection have <span style="color:#008080">˚wr[</span>, the passive participle here, instead of <span style="color:#008080">˚r[</span>.”

<span style="color:#800000">ftb87</span> “Sentant tous ses sens engloutis d’une majeste et resplendeur infinie, que sa veue pouvoit porter.” — *Fr*.

<span style="color:#800000">ftb88</span> The objections to this interpretation are,

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1">**1.** That the verb <span style="color:#008080">hrk</span> *carah*, here used, does not mean *to bore*, but that the radical idea of the word is, *to dig*, *to hollow out*; as to dig a well, Genesis 26:25; a pit, Psalm 7:15; to carve or cut out a sepulcher from a rock, 2 Chronicles 16:14; and hence we find it transferred from the grottoes of the sepulcher to the quarry of human nature, Isaiah 51:1, 2. Williams, viewing the verb as properly signifying *digged*, *carved*, or *cut out*, in the sense *of forming*, explains the words as if the Psalmist had said, “Mine ears hast thou made, or *prepared*, for the most exact and complete obedience.” Stuart, (Commentary on Hebrews 10:5,) and Davidson, (Sacred Hermeneutics, p. 461,) viewing the word as meaning *digged*, *hollowed out*, simply in the sense of *opening*, read, “Mine ears hast thou opened;” which they explain as meaning, Thou hast made me obedient, or, I am entirely devoted to thy service; observing, that *to open* or *uncover the ear* was a customary expression among the Hebrews, to</p>

<!-- PAGE 1257 -->

signify a revealing something to any one, including the idea of listening to the communication, followed by prompt obedience, Isaiah 50:5; 1 Samuel. 20:2. There is another verb of the same radical letters, which means to *purchase* or *provide*; and this is the sense in which the LXX. understood <span style="color:#008080">hrk</span>, *carah*, as is evident from their rendering it by <span style="color:#0000d4">κατηρτίσω</span>.

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1">**2.** That the verb used in Exodus is not <span style="color:#008080">hrk</span>, as here, but <span style="color:#008080">[xr</span>, *ratsang*.</p>

<p style="margin-left:2em;" markdown="1">**3.** That only one ear was pierced, as appears from the passages in the Pentateuch in which the rite is described. But here the plural number is used, denoting both ears. From these considerations, it is concluded that there is here no allusion to the custom of boring the ear of a servant under the Law.</p>

<span style="color:#800000">ftb89</span> This is the literal rendering of the Hebrew, and means, As dear to me as life itself; (John 6:38; Job 38:36.)

<span style="color:#800000">ftb90</span> Anciently, books did not consist, like ours, of a number of distinct leaves bound together, but were composed of sheets of parchment joined to each other, and rolled up for preservation upon wooden rollers, as our charts of geography are; and in this form are all the sacred MSS. of the Jewish synagogues to this day. The *roll of the book*, therefore, simply means *the book* itself. With respect to the reading of the Septuagint, “<span style="color:#0000d4">En κεθαλίσδι βιβλίου</span>;” — “In the head of the book;” and which Paul, in Hebrews 10:7, quotes instead of the Hebrew: this is an expression which the LXX. employ simply to mean *the book*, as in Ezra 6:2; Ezekiel 2:9; and 3:1-3; and not the *beginning* or *head of the book*. At the extremity of the cylinder on which the Hebrew <span style="color:#008080">rpk</span>, <span style="color:#0000d4">bibliou</span>, book or manuscript, was rolled, were *heads* or *knobs* for the sake of convenience to those who used the MS. The *knob* or *head*, <span style="color:#0000d4">keqaliv</span>, is here taken as a *part* put for the *whole*. <span style="color:#0000d4">Keqaliv βιβλίου</span> means therefore <span style="color:#0000d4">bibliou</span>, or <span style="color:#008080">rps</span>, with a <span style="color:#0000d4">keqaliv</span>, i.e., a manuscript roll. *— Stuart on Hebrews* 10:7.
